We selected N=512 as the reference grid with P=1.

W=\frac{(N-1)^2}{P}
\]

\[
W_1=511^2=261121
\]

Therefore:
\[
N=1+\operatorname{round}(511\sqrt P)
\]

Selected grid sizes
P	N
1	512
2	724
3	886
4	1023
5	1144
6	1253

Weak scaling efficiency is:
\[
E_{\text{weak}}(P)=
\frac{T_1}{T_P}\times100
\]

Unlike strong scaling efficiency, we do not divide by P.
Successful measurements
P	N	Strips median (s)	Blocks median (s)
1	512	0.006149141	0.005633556
2	724	0.007777280	0.008710041
3	886	Three runs completed with Amer; verify raw logs	Pending
4	1023	Pending	Pending
5	1144	Pending	Pending
6	1253	Pending	Pending

For P=2:
- Strips weak scaling efficiency: approximately 79.07%.
- Blocks weak scaling efficiency: approximately 64.68%.
Three additional strips repetitions for N=886, P=3 were successfully saved by the automated script using Mahmoud and Amer.

Some screenshot timings were partially clipped, so the complete values should be extracted from the actual raw logs rather than guessed.
Remaining weak scaling experiments

We have 15 confirmed measurements out of 36, including reused P=1 results.

Twenty-one runs remain, subject to verification of the saved logs.

12. Part 4 — Communication share
The communication metric is:
\[
C=\frac{t_{\text{comm}}}{t_{\text{total}}}
\times100
\]

For example, strips with N=512 and P=4 spent approximately 99.88% of the measured execution time in communication and associated waiting.
Results for N=512
P	Strips communication	Blocks communication
1	0.04%	0.06%
2	14.66%	21.92%
3	98.69%	97.75%
4	99.88%	99.70%
5	99.76%	99.49%


StripsBlocks

0%25%50%75%100%12345















Communication increases dramatically once processes are distributed across multiple VMs.

Important technical detail: the solver reports independent MPI maximum reductions for total, computation and communication times. These maxima may come from different ranks. Consequently, communication share is an overhead indicator, not necessarily an exact time breakdown of a particular process.

13. Part 4 — Theoretical performance model
We combined Part 0 network measurements with Part 4 computation rates.

The general model is:
\[
T_{\text{step}}=
T_{\text{comp}}+T_{\text{comm}}
\]

For homogeneous processors:
\[
T_{\text{comp}}\approx
\frac{(N-1)^2}{P}t_c
\]

The refined model accounts for:
- Different computation speeds for each VM.
- Actual domain decomposition.
- Physical boundaries.
- Neighboring ranks.
- Process placement.

1)CASE V: exact solution: near the center sin sin

Why are t_total and t_comp equal?
Because Part 1 uses one process. The program times the computation loop, and there is no MPI communication inside it.


Why is t_comm=0?
Because no temperatures are exchanged between machines. That communication will be needed in our parallel implementation.


2)CASE 1: edges
./heat --decomp serial --case 1 --N 64 --r 0.2 --steps 100 --out results/demo_case1.bin

t_total and t_comp measure how long the computer takes to execute the simulation, in seconds.

t_total — Total execution time: time spent inside the simulation's time-stepping loop.
t_comp — Computation time: time spent calculating new temperatures using our heat equation:

T	Total simulated time for all steps

In Case 1, all four boundaries are fixed at temperature 1. Heat spreads inward from every direction, creating a hot border around a cold center.

In Case 2, only the top and bottom boundaries are hot. The left and right boundaries remain cold. Therefore, the heatmap shows two hot horizontal regions, while the center stays cold after 100 steps.

Both cases use the same numerical method, grid size and time-step parameter. The different results come entirely from their boundary conditions.

    Parallel 2D Heat Diffusion with MPI over Tailscale


Contents

1 Overview                                                                                  1

1    Overview

In this project your team builds a small distributed-memory cluster out of your own computers
and uses it to simulate heat spreading across a square plate. Each team member runs an Ubuntu
virtual machine; the virtual machines are joined into one private network with Tailscale; and
your solver runs across all of them with MPI.
You will write everything yourselves, starting from the numerical scheme you derived in the
background notes (Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation). The project has the following
parts:

Part 0. Build the cluster. Virtual machines, Tailscale, SSH, Open MPI, and a first measurement
        of what your network can do.

Part 1. Serial solver. A correct, verified C++ implementation of your explicit scheme.

Part 2. 1D strip decomposition. The first parallel version, with halo exchange between
        neighbouring processes.



                                              1
Part 3. 2D block decomposition. A Cartesian process grid and halo exchange in four
        directions.

Part 4. Performance study. Measure, model and explain how your solver scales on your
        cluster.

Part 5. Advanced (optional): GPU computing with CUDA. Each machine computes its
        part of the plate on its own NVIDIA GPU, while MPI still connects the machines.

  Requirements — background notes
 Every team must complete all the Tasks in the background notes (Numerical Methods
 for the Heat Equation), except those marked optional, and hand in their written solutions.
 Your solver is built on the results of these Tasks, in particular:

           Task       Used in
           3.2, 3.4   second derivative and discrete Laplacian (Part 1)
           5.2        the update rule your solver implements (Parts 1–3, 5)
           5.5        the accuracy you must observe in the convergence study (Part 1)
           6.3        the stability limit your program enforces (Section 3)
           7.2        the convergence study (Part 1)
           8.2        the communication model for strips and blocks (Parts 3 and 4)

 Hand in the solutions as a separate PDF (typed, or neatly handwritten and scanned), numbered
 as in the notes. Tasks that ask for a program or a plot, such as 3.5, 4.1 or 6.7, are answered
 with your results and a short explanation.


Learning objectives

By the end of the project you should be able to:

 set up and debug an MPI environment across several machines;

 decompose a grid-based computation and implement halo exchange;

 verify that a parallel program is correct, not just that it runs;

 measure latency, bandwidth and run time carefully and reproducibly;

 build a simple performance model and use it to explain your measurements, including the cases
  where adding machines makes things slower.

 An honest warning about speed-ups
 Your cluster is connected by home and campus networks through an encrypted overlay. Its
 latency is a thousand times or more worse than that of a real supercomputer interconnect.
 For small grids, your parallel code will very likely run slower on several machines than on one.
 That is not a failure: explaining exactly when and why it happens is one of the main goals of
 the project, and it is worth as much as a good speed-up.

Script completed

Output:

### Numerical_Solutions_Simple.docx pages 4 images 2 equations 0 lines 99
Numerical Background
Part 1 Solutions:
Task 3.2 - Second derivative
(a) We approximate the second derivative by using the values at the left, center and right points.
D₂f(x) = [f(x − h) − 2f(x) + f(x + h)] / h²
We use the Taylor expansions below. The first-derivative terms cancel when we add them.
f(x + h) = f(x) + hf′(x) + (h²/2)f″(x) + (h³/6)f‴(x) + …
f(x − h) = f(x) − hf′(x) + (h²/2)f″(x) − (h³/6)f‴(x) + …
Adding and rearranging gives the central second-difference formula above.
(b) The leading error is:
D₂f(x) − f″(x) = (h² / 12) f⁽⁴⁾(x) + O(h⁴)
Therefore the approximation is second-order accurate: its main error is proportional to h².
(c) For f(x) = sin(x) at x = 1, the exact value is f″(1) = −sin(1).
h | Absolute error | Error ratio
0.1 | 0.000700992 | -
0.05 | 0.000175292 | 3.999
0.025 | 0.000043826 | 4.000
0.0125 | 0.000010957 | 4.000
When h is halved, the error is about four times smaller. This agrees with second-order accuracy.
Task 3.4 - Discrete Laplacian
(a) Horizontal second derivative:
uₓₓ ≈ [uᵢ₋₁,ⱼ − 2uᵢ,ⱼ + uᵢ₊₁,ⱼ] / (Δx)²
(b) Vertical second derivative:
uᵧᵧ ≈ [uᵢ,ⱼ₋₁ − 2uᵢ,ⱼ + uᵢ,ⱼ₊₁] / (Δy)²
(c) Add the two formulas to approximate the Laplacian, ∇²u = uₓₓ + uᵧᵧ. The error is O((Δx)² + (Δy)²).
(d) When Δx = Δy = h, we obtain the five-point formula:
∇²ₕuᵢ,ⱼ ≈ [Left + Right + Top + Bottom − 4 × Center] / h²
(e) The stencil uses the center and four neighbors. Its weights, before division by h², are:
                         +1
                +1       −4       +1
                         +1
(f) If the neighbor average is larger than the center value, the Laplacian is positive and heat tends to raise the center temperature. This agrees with the mean-value interpretation.
Task 5.2 - Update rule for the heat equation
The heat equation is uₜ = α(uₓₓ + uᵧᵧ). We use Forward Euler for time and the discrete Laplacian for space.
(a) For different horizontal and vertical spacings:
uⁿ⁺¹ᵢ,ⱼ = uⁿᵢ,ⱼ + αΔt [ (Left − 2Center + Right)/(Δx)²
                                 + (Bottom − 2Center + Top)/(Δy)² ]
(b) With equal spacing h, define r = αΔt/h². Then:
New center = (1 − 4r) × Center + r × (Left + Right + Top + Bottom)
This is the equation implemented in our C++ serial solver.
(c) The space-time stencil uses five values at old time n to calculate one center value at new time n + 1:
Time n:   Left, Right, Top, Bottom, Center   →   Time n + 1: New center
Fixed boundary nodes are not updated.
(d) With coefficients already calculated, one update needs about 6 floating-point operations, 5 input values and 1 output value. Actual memory traffic can be lower because neighboring values are reused from cache.
Task 5.5 - Accuracy and consistency
Our method makes two types of approximation: Forward Euler in time and central differences in space.
Forward Euler is first-order in time. The spatial formulas are second-order. So the local truncation error is:
τ = O(Δt) + O((Δx)²) + O((Δy)²)
Therefore p = 1 and q = 2.
The method is consistent because the truncation error approaches zero when Δt, Δx and Δy approach zero. Consistency alone does not guarantee stability.
Task 6.3 - Stability condition
(a) Von Neumann analysis studies how a Fourier-shaped numerical error changes after one time step. Write the error as Gⁿ exp[i(jθ + kφ)]. Substituting it into the update rule gives:
G(θ, φ) = 1 − 4rₓ sin²(θ/2) − 4rᵧ sin²(φ/2)
rₓ = αΔt/(Δx)²,      rᵧ = αΔt/(Δy)²
(b) A stable scheme requires |G| ≤ 1 for every error mode. The worst high-frequency mode is the alternating checkerboard (θ = φ = π). Its factor is:
Gworst = 1 − 4rₓ − 4rᵧ
Requiring Gworst ≥ −1 gives the general stability condition:
αΔt [1/(Δx)² + 1/(Δy)²] ≤ 1/2
(c) If Δx = Δy = h, then rₓ = rᵧ = r, so:
Gworst = 1 − 8r        and        0 ≤ r ≤ 0.25
(d) For equal spacing, the stability limits are r ≤ 1/2 in 1D, r ≤ 1/4 in 2D and r ≤ 1/6 in 3D.
Task 6.7 - Deliberate instability test
We deliberately exceeded the 2D stability bound by 5%: r = 1.05 × 0.25 = 0.2625, with N = 16 and Case V.
For the worst mode, G = 1 − 8(0.2625) = −1.1. Its sign changes from step to step and its magnitude grows by 10% per step.
Time steps | Maximum absolute error
100 | 0.001854518
300 | 0.0000954815
400 | 0.000607306
500 | 1.303888
600 | 2,822.024
1,000 | 6.192109 × 10¹⁶
The error becomes very large after about 400-500 steps. The 1,000-step image shows alternating positive and negative values, called a checkerboard pattern.
Measured unstable temperature field after 1,000 steps (r = 0.2625).
The strongest oscillations in this final image are near the center. To identify exactly where instability first became visible, the intermediate saved grids still need to be inspected.
Task 7.1 - Expected convergence order
We know that the error behaves as O(Δt) + O(h²). During our tests we kept r fixed, so Δt = rh²/α.
Therefore the total leading error behaves as O(h²). We expect second-order convergence: halving h should make the error about four times smaller.
Task 7.2 - Convergence study
(a) We ran the serial solver for Case V with T = 0.05 and r = 0.2, at N = 16, 32, 64, 128 and 256.
The exact solution is u(x, y, t) = sin(πx) sin(πy) exp(−2π²t).
(b) The recorded errors were:
N | h | Steps | Maximum error | L2,h error | Order
16 | 1/16 | 64 | 0.001663371 | 0.000831685 | -
32 | 1/32 | 256 | 0.000414182 | 0.000207091 | 2.0058
64 | 1/64 | 1,024 | 0.000103442 | 0.0000517212 | 2.0014
128 | 1/128 | 4,096 | 0.0000258542 | 0.0000129271 | 2.0004
256 | 1/256 | 16,384 | 0.00000646314 | 0.00000323157 | 2.0001
The observed order is calculated by:

### Part1_Report_Section_and_Appendices.docx pages 4 images 3 equations 0 lines 58
Here r = Δt / h². The explicit method is stable for 0 < r ≤ 0.25. When a final time T is requested, the solver adjusts the number of steps and Δt so that the final time is reached without exceeding the requested r.
The C++17 solver uses two contiguous double arrays, a single row-major indexing function and an array swap after each iteration. Boundary values are held fixed. It builds with one Makefile command using -O2 and -ffp-contract=off. std::chrono::steady_clock measures only the update loop, excluding initialization and file output. The program prints a one-line key=value summary and, when requested, writes the full (N+1) × (N+1) grid as raw doubles in row-major order.
Cases 1 and 2 were checked at N = 8, r = 0.2 and 20 steps. Both produced finite temperatures between 0 and 1 with the prescribed edge values. For Case V, the exact solution is sin(πx)sin(πy)exp(−2π²t). We report the maximum error and the discrete L2,h error at the final time.
The convergence study used T = 0.05, r = 0.2 and N = 16, 32, 64, 128 and 256. Doubling N reduced both error norms by approximately four, with observed orders close to 2 (2.0058 to 2.0001 for L2,h). This matches the expected O(Δt + h²) accuracy, since Δt = rh². The complete table and log-log plot are in Appendix D.
The normal solver rejects r > 0.25. A separate test program deliberately used r = 0.2625 (5% above the limit). The maximum error grew from 6.07 × 10⁻⁴ at 400 steps to 1.30 at 500 steps and 6.19 × 10¹⁶ at 1,000 steps. The final field shows alternating positive and negative temperatures in a checkerboard pattern, consistent with the unstable high-frequency amplification factor G = 1 − 8r = −1.1. Figures and data appear in Appendix E.
The verification initial condition is u(x,y,0) = sin(πx)sin(πy), with zero temperature on all four boundaries. For α = 1, the exact solution is u(x,y,T) = sin(πx)sin(πy)exp(−2π²T). Runs used T = 0.05 and requested r = 0.2. The time-step count increases fourfold whenever N doubles.
N | Steps | Maximum error | Discrete L2,h error | Order (L2,h)
Maximum error:  ||e||∞ = max |u(numerical) − u(exact)|. Discrete L2,h error:  ||e||2,h = h × sqrt(Σ e(i,j)²), summed over all grid nodes. Numerical values were recorded from the serial Case V runs on heat-2 on 23 November 2026.
Appendix E. Deliberate instability test
The error is still small at 300 steps, becomes visible around 400 steps, and grows rapidly by 500 steps. We reproduced the saved intermediate fields from the experimental source to inspect where the error grows; the largest errors are in the plate interior, near the center. These snapshots do not establish an exact first unstable iteration.
Figure E1. Numerical error relative to the exact solution at 400 and 500 steps. Each image has its own scale.
Figure E2. Measured temperature field at 1,000 steps. The values reach approximately ±6 × 10¹⁶ and alternate in a checkerboard pattern.
Representative commands used on the heat-2 Ubuntu VM (the output directory must exist before writing files):
./heat --decomp serial --case 1 --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 20 --out case1.bin
./heat --decomp serial --case 2 --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 20 --out case2.bin
./heat --decomp serial --case V --N 16 --r 0.26 --steps 20
./unstable_demo --case V --N 16 --r 0.2625 --steps 1000 --out results/instability_1000.bin
### Part2_Report_Simple_Copy_Paste.docx pages 4 images 4 equations 0 lines 51
We implemented a parallel version of the 2D heat solver using MPI. The goal was to divide the calculations between processes while keeping the final temperature grid identical to the serial solver from Part 1.
The N − 1 interior rows are divided into horizontal strips. If the division is uneven, the lowest ranks receive one extra row. Rank 0 owns the bottom strip. Each process stores its own rows plus two ghost rows, so its local grid contains (nlocal + 2) × (N + 1) values. All processes use the same five-point heat update as the serial solver:
Before every time step, each process exchanges its first and last owned rows with neighboring ranks using MPI_Sendrecv. The ghost rows provide the temperatures needed at strip interfaces. Each message contains N + 1 contiguous doubles; MPI_PROC_NULL is used when there is no neighboring rank. After the computation, MPI_Gatherv collects the rows on rank 0, which writes the same binary grid format as the serial solver.
Using MPI_Wtime, the program measures the stepping loop, computation and halo communication. MPI_Reduce with MPI_MAX reports the maximum time across ranks for each measurement. Setup, output and error checking are excluded from the timed loop.
We checked Cases 1, 2 and V using N = 8, 10 and 16, P = 1, 2, 3 and 4, r = 0.2, and 10 steps. All 36 local MPI runs matched the serial output bit for bit using cmp. The professor’s halo-debug tests also passed for N = 8, Case 2, one step and P = 2 or 3, including the uneven division. The MPI solver was then tested across two VMs and three VMs. Both distributed Case 2 outputs matched the serial result bit for bit (N = 8, 10 steps).
Single VM (36 comparisons) | Cases 1, 2, V; N = 8, 10, 16; P = 1–4; 10 steps | 36/36 identical
Halo debug | Case 2; N = 8; P = 2 and 3; 1 step | Ghost rows verified; identical
Two-VM distributed | Case 2; N = 8; P = 2; 10 steps | Identical
Three-VM distributed | Case 2; N = 8; P = 3; 10 steps | Identical
E. Pending optional verification — three-VM Case V
Build: make (mpic++ -std=c++17 -O2 -ffp-contract=off). Use --decomp strips, --case 1|2|V, --N, --r, --steps or --T, and --out. Compare the resulting binary with the corresponding serial grid using cmp. For distributed runs, the hostfile places one rank on each selected VM and Open MPI is configured to use tailscale0.

3.8    A warning about round-off
Truncation error suggests that smaller h is always better. In floating point arithmetic it is not.
Each value f (x ± h) carries a relative rounding error of about ε (≈ 10−16 in double precision).
In a difference quotient these small errors are divided by h (first derivatives) or h2 (second
derivatives). For the forward difference the total error behaves roughly like

                                           h ′′     2ε|f |
                                             |f | +        ,
                                           2          h

 Task 5.5. (Accuracy of your scheme)
 Using your truncation error results from Chapter 3 and from the analysis of forward Euler,
 show that the local truncation error of your 2D scheme has the form

Chapter 6

Stability and Convergence

6.1    Consistency is not enough
A consistent scheme reproduces the PDE as the grid is refined, but this alone does not guarantee
that its solution approaches the PDE’s solution. Small errors (truncation errors, round-off) are
made at every node at every step. If the scheme amplifies them, they grow exponentially and
swamp the answer, exactly as forward Euler did on the test equation with λ∆t > 2.

  Definition 6.1. (Stability and convergence)
 A scheme is stable if the size of its numerical solution (in some norm) is bounded in terms
 of the size of its initial data, uniformly for all sufficiently small ∆t, ∆x, ∆y satisfying any
 stated restriction between them. It is convergent if the numerical solution at any fixed time
 T tends to the exact solution as the grid is refined.

 Theorem 6.2. (Lax equivalence theorem)
  For a consistent finite difference scheme approximating a well-posed linear initial value
  problem,
                                  stability ⇐⇒ convergence.

   The theorem turns a hard question (does the scheme converge?) into two easier ones: is it
consistent (Task 5.5), and is it stable (this chapter)?

## Performance Observations

For small grids, MPI communication overhead is significantly larger than computation time.

Distributed execution over Tailscale can therefore be slower than serial execution, particularly when network latency is high.

The main objective of Part 2 is numerical correctness rather than speedup.

## Network Limitations

The original three-VM Case V experiment with 64 time steps was interrupted by intermittent network connectivity failures and MPI daemon disconnections.

A shorter experiment with 10 time steps was subsequently completed successfully across all three VMs.

The successful shorter experiment demonstrated bit-for-bit agreement with the serial solver. The original 64-step distributed experiment remains unverified.

Network connectivity should be checked before distributed demonstrations.

## Deadlock Prevention

The solver uses MPI_Sendrecv to exchange neighboring rows safely.

Separate blocking MPI_Send operations followed by MPI_Recv may cause deadlock when processes wait for matching receive operations.

MPI_Sendrecv combines sending and receiving in one operation and avoids this communication-ordering problem.

MPI_PROC_NULL is used for processes located at the physical boundaries of the plate.

## Final Verification Status

| Requirement | Status |
|---|---|
| Balanced strip decomposition | Passed |
| Ghost-row allocation | Passed |
| MPI_Sendrecv halo exchange | Passed |
| MPI_PROC_NULL handling | Passed |
| Halo debugging with P=2 | Passed |
| Halo debugging with P=3 | Passed |
| Cases 1, 2 and V | Passed locally |
| 36 automated correctness tests | 36/36 passed |
| Two-VM distributed Case 2 | Passed |
| Three-VM distributed Case 2 | Passed |

def montage(tiles,cap,path):
 w,h,cols=cap
 out=Image.new('RGB',(w*cols,h*((len(tiles)+cols-1)//cols)),'#dddddd')
 dr=ImageDraw.Draw(out)
 for i,(n,im) in enumerate(tiles):
  x,y=i%cols*w,i//cols*h
  im.thumbnail((w-12,h-28))
  if im.mode!='RGB': im=im.convert('RGB')
  out.paste(im,(x+(w-im.width)//2,y+21))
  dr.text((x+5,y+4),n,fill='black')
 out.save(path)

montage(tiles,(340,470,4),t/'heat_mpi_audit_guides.png')

F. Reproducibility
Build: make (mpic++ -std=c++17 -O2 -ffp-contract=off). Use --decomp strips, --case 1|2|V, --N, --r, --steps or --T, and --out. Compare the resulting binary with the corresponding serial grid using cmp. For distributed runs, the hostfile places one rank on each selected VM and Open MPI is configured to use tailscale0.
Sources: Project specification, Section 6 (Part 2: 1D strip decomposition); Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation, Section 8.4 and Task 8.2; tested Part 2 strips.cpp and terminal results from 23 November 2026.


The Python analysis script uses the median of the small-message one-way times (m ≤ 1,024 bytes) as the estimate of t_s. Fixing t_s, it estimates the transfer coefficient with the measurements m ≥ 1 MiB: t_w = sum[m(t(m) − t_s)] / sum(m²). Bandwidth is 1 / (10⁶ t_w) in decimal MB/s. The model comes from Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation, Section 8.4; here m is counted in bytes.
The cross-VM plots show unusually large variation at higher message sizes. These results represent one complete ten-round-trip batch per connection, not independent rerun distributions. For final interpretation, avoid describing these estimates as hardware limits.

## main...origin/main
# Part 1 — Serial heat solver (Step 2 working version)

This folder contains the active serial solver from the team project, with a minimal interface/output update. The numerical stencil, initialization, boundary cases, time-step selection, error calculations, and binary output are unchanged from the supplied `src/serial.cpp`.

## Build (Ubuntu / Linux)

```bash
make
```

This builds `./heat` using C++17, `-O2`, and `-ffp-contract=off`.

## Run

```bash
./heat --decomp serial --case V --N 16 --r 0.2 --T 0.05 --out verification.bin
./heat --decomp serial --case 1 --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 10
./heat --decomp serial --case 2 --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 1 --debug
```

Supported options: `--decomp serial`, `--case 1|2|V`, `--N`, `--r`, `--T` *or* `--steps`, `--out`, `--debug`, `--help`.

Only a **single `key=value` summary line** goes to standard output on successful runs. `--debug` prints the full grid to standard error. `t_total` is the step-loop duration in this serial program and equals `t_comp`; `t_comm=0`. Setup and binary output are excluded from the timing.

`--out` writes the `(N+1) x (N+1)` field as native binary doubles (little-endian on the course's x86-64 Ubuntu VMs), with `i` varying fastest.

## Step 2 change scope

- Accept the `--decomp serial` argument required by the common interface.
- Reject other decomposition modes in this **serial-only** executable.
- Build `./heat` in one command rather than `src/serial`.
- Print only one machine-readable summary on stdout, including `t_total`.
- Make full-grid printing opt-in via `--debug` (stderr).

The final project-wide `heat` integration with MPI strips and blocks is a later step. Convergence, instability scripts and retained results from the teammate's original archive will be brought into the final Part 1 directory in later steps.

## Verification performed when preparing this version

- `make` builds successfully with the flags above.
- Case V (`N=16`, `r=0.2`, `T=0.05`): field bytes identical to the original.
- Case 2 (`N=8`, `r=0.2`, three steps): field bytes identical to the original.
- Unsupported `--decomp blocks` and unstable `r=0.26` are rejected.

Keep the original archive as a backup. No claims are made here about a fresh three-VM MPI run; this file is the serial solver only.

CXX = g++
CXXFLAGS = -std=c++17 -O2 -ffp-contract=off

all: heat

heat: src/serial.cpp
	$(CXX) $(CXXFLAGS) src/serial.cpp -o heat

# Convenient alias for building the serial implementation.
serial: heat

clean:
	rm -f heat

.PHONY: all serial clean


import struct
import matplotlib

matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

N = 16
width = N + 1

# Read the unstable grid from the binary file
with open("results/instability_1000.bin", "rb") as f:
    data = f.read()

values = struct.unpack("<" + str(width * width) + "d", data)

# Convert the flat array to a 2D grid
grid = [
    values[j * width:(j + 1) * width]
    for j in range(width)
]

# Create the heatmap
plt.figure(figsize=(7, 6))

plt.imshow(
    grid,
    origin="lower",
    extent=[0, 1, 0, 1],
    cmap="seismic",
    interpolation="nearest"
)

plt.colorbar(label="Numerical temperature")
plt.xlabel("x")
plt.ylabel("y")
plt.title("Numerical Instability - r=0.2625, N=16, 1000 steps")

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig("results/instability.png", dpi=200)

print("Saved: results/instability.png")

import csv
import math
from pathlib import Path

import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

results = Path("results")
rows = []

# Read the actual output of the five solver runs
for line in (results / "convergence_runs.txt").read_text().splitlines():
    if not line.startswith("decomp=serial"):
        continue

    values = dict(item.split("=", 1) for item in line.split() if "=" in item)
    N = int(values["N"])

    rows.append({
        "N": N,
        "h": 1.0 / N,
        "steps": int(values["steps"]),
        "max_error": float(values["err_inf"]),
        "l2h_error": float(values["err_l2h"]),
    })

rows.sort(key=lambda row: row["N"])

assert [row["N"] for row in rows] == [16, 32, 64, 128, 256]

# Calculate observed order of convergence
for i, row in enumerate(rows):
    row["p_obs"] = "" if i == 0 else math.log2(
        rows[i-1]["l2h_error"] / row["l2h_error"]
    )

# Save the completed table
with (results / "convergence.csv").open("w", newline="") as file:
    writer = csv.DictWriter(file, fieldnames=rows[0].keys())
    writer.writeheader()
    writer.writerows(rows)

# Plot measured error and theoretical second-order reference
h = [row["h"] for row in rows]
errors = [row["l2h_error"] for row in rows]
reference = [errors[0] * (x / h[0])**2 for x in h]

plt.figure(figsize=(7, 5))
plt.loglog(h, errors, "o-", label="Measured L2 error")
plt.loglog(h, reference, "--", label="Reference: order 2")
plt.xlabel("Grid spacing h")
plt.ylabel("L2 error")
plt.title("Serial Heat Solver - Convergence")
plt.grid(True, which="both", alpha=0.3)
plt.legend()
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig(results / "convergence.png", dpi=200)

print("N     Observed order")
for row in rows:
    p = row["p_obs"]
    print(f'{row["N"]:<5} {p:.4f}' if p != "" else f'{row["N"]:<5} -')

print("Saved results/convergence.csv")
print("Saved results/convergence.png")

const p=String.raw`
from pathlib import Path
from zipfile import ZipFile
from io import BytesIO
import csv
import math
import re
from PIL import Image
from docx import Document
from docx.enum.table import WD_TABLE_ALIGNMENT, WD_CELL_VERTICAL_ALIGNMENT
from docx.enum.text import WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH
from docx.oxml import OxmlElement
from docx.oxml.ns import qn
from docx.shared import Inches, Pt, RGBColor

repo = Path.cwd()
results = repo / 'part1' / 'results'
output = repo / 'output' / 'part1'
output.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
reference = Path('C:/Users/User/Desktop/Part1_Report_Section_and_Appendices.docx')
with ZipFile(reference) as package:
    convergence_plot = package.read('word/media/image1.png')
    intermediate_plot = package.read('word/media/image2.png')
    final_plot = package.read('word/media/image3.png')

with (results / 'convergence.csv').open(newline='', encoding='utf-8') as source:
    convergence = list(csv.DictReader(source))

instability = []
for steps in (100, 300, 400, 500, 600, 1000):
    log = (results / f'instability_{steps}.txt').read_text(encoding='utf-8')
    max_error = float(re.search(r'^Max error = (\S+)', log, re.M).group(1))
    instability.append((steps, max_error))
rendered_errors = ('0.00185452', '0.00009548', '0.00060731', '1.303888', '2,822.024', '6.192109 × 10¹⁶')
assert all(round(e, 8) == e for _, e in instability[:3])

def node(parent, tag, **attrs):
    elm = OxmlElement('w:' + tag)
    for attr, value in attrs.items():
        elm.set(qn('w:' + attr), str(value))
    parent.append(elm)
    return elm

def new_document(title):
    doc = Document()
    sec = doc.sections[0]
    sec.page_width, sec.page_height = Inches(8.27), Inches(11.69)
    sec.left_margin, sec.right_margin = Inches(.73), Inches(.73)
    sec.top_margin, sec.bottom_margin = Inches(.66), Inches(.66)
    styles = doc.styles
    for name, font, size, bold in [
        ('Normal', 'Aptos', 10.5, False),
        ('Title', 'Aptos', 16, True),
        ('Heading 1', 'Aptos', 12, True),
        ('Heading 2', 'Aptos', 10.5, True),
        ('Caption', 'Aptos', 9, False),
    ]:
        style = styles[name]
        style.font.name = font
        style.font.size = Pt(size)
        style.font.bold = bold
        style.font.italic = False
        style.font.color.rgb = RGBColor(0, 0, 0)
        form = style.paragraph_format
        form.line_spacing = 1.12 if name == 'Normal' else 1.05
        form.space_before = Pt(7 if name.startswith('Heading') else 0)
        form.space_after = Pt(6 if name == 'Normal' else 4)
        form.widow_control = True
        if name in ('Title', 'Heading 1', 'Heading 2'):
            form.keep_with_next = True
            form.keep_together = True
        style_ppr = style.element.get_or_add_pPr()
        for border in style_ppr.findall(qn('w:pBdr')):
            style_ppr.remove(border)
    doc.core_properties.title = title
    doc.core_properties.author = ''
    return doc

def body(doc, text, keep_next=False):
    paragraph = doc.add_paragraph(text)
    paragraph.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = keep_next
    return paragraph

def heading(doc, text, page=False, level=1):
    paragraph = doc.add_heading(text, level=level)
    if page:
        paragraph.paragraph_format.page_break_before = True
    return paragraph

def equation(doc, text, keep_next=False):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.paragraph_format.left_indent = Inches(.19)
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(5)
    p.paragraph_format.keep_together = True
    p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = keep_next
    run = p.add_run(text)
    run.font.name = 'Cambria Math'
    return p

def caption(doc, text, before=False):
    p = doc.add_paragraph(text, 'Caption')
    p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = before
    p.paragraph_format.space_before = Pt(6 if before else 2)
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(4 if before else 8)
    return p

def data_table(doc, headers, rows, widths):
    table = doc.add_table(rows=0, cols=len(headers))
    table.autofit = False
    table.alignment = WD_TABLE_ALIGNMENT.CENTER
    for column, width in zip(table.columns, widths):
        column.width = Inches(width)
    props = table._tbl.tblPr
    borders = node(props, 'tblBorders')
    for edge in ('top', 'left', 'bottom', 'right', 'insideH', 'insideV'):
        node(borders, edge, val='single', sz=5, color='D9D9D9')
    margins = node(props, 'tblCellMar')
    for edge in ('top', 'bottom', 'left', 'right'):
        node(margins, edge, w=43 if edge in ('top', 'bottom') else 76, type='dxa')
    for idx, row in enumerate([headers, *rows]):
        wr = table.add_row()
        row_props = wr._tr.get_or_add_trPr()
        node(row_props, 'cantSplit')
        if idx == 0:
            node(row_props, 'tblHeader')
        for col, (cell, value) in enumerate(zip(wr.cells, row)):
            cell.width = Inches(widths[col])
            cell.vertical_alignment = WD_CELL_VERTICAL_ALIGNMENT.CENTER
            if idx == 0:
                node(cell._tc.get_or_add_tcPr(), 'shd', fill='F2F2F2')
            p = cell.paragraphs[0]
            p.alignment = WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.CENTER if idx == 0 else WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.RIGHT
            p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(0)
            p.paragraph_format.line_spacing = 1.04
            p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = idx == 0
            run = p.add_run(str(value))
            run.bold = idx == 0
            run.font.size = Pt(9.5)
    return table

def figure(doc, source, width, description, alt):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.alignment = WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.CENTER
    p.paragraph_format.space_before = Pt(6)
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(0)
    p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = True
    data = BytesIO(source) if isinstance(source, bytes) else str(source)
    picture = p.add_run().add_picture(data, width=Inches(width))
    picture._inline.docPr.set('descr', alt)
    caption(doc, description)

def commands(doc, text):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(8)
    p.paragraph_format.keep_together = True
    p.paragraph_format.line_spacing = 1.06
    run = p.add_run(text)
    run.font.name = 'Consolas'
    run.font.size = Pt(9)

def sci(number, digits=5):
    significand, exponent = f'{number:.{digits}e}'.split('e')
    exp = str(int(exponent)).translate(str.maketrans('-0123456789', '⁻⁰¹²³⁴⁵⁶⁷⁸⁹'))
    return f'{significand} × 10{exp}'

def convergence_table(doc, label):
    caption(doc, label, before=True)
    rows = [
        (row['N'], f"1/{row['N']}", f"{int(row['steps']):,}", sci(float(row['max_error'])),
         sci(float(row['l2h_error'])), f"{float(row['p_obs']):.4f}" if row['p_obs'] else '—')
        for row in convergence
    ]
    data_table(doc, ('N', 'h', 'Steps', 'Maximum error', 'Discrete L₂ error', 'Observed order'),
               rows, (.53, .64, .78, 1.50, 1.63, 1.28))

def instability_table(doc, label):
    caption(doc, label, before=True)
    rows = [(f'{steps:,}', spelling) for (steps, _), spelling in zip(instability, rendered_errors)]
    data_table(doc, ('Steps', 'Maximum absolute error'), rows, (1.48, 2.62))

# The first page is the report section. The appendix titles continue after Part 0.
report = new_document('Part 1 Serial heat solver and verification')
report.add_heading('Part 1 Serial heat solver and verification', 0)
body(report, 'We implemented and verified a serial solver for heat diffusion on a unit square. The Case V errors decreased by a factor of about four each time the grid spacing was halved. A separate test above the stability limit produced a growing checkerboard pattern.')
heading(report, 'Numerical method')
body(report, 'We use N intervals along each axis, so the spacing is h = 1/N and the grid contains (N + 1)² nodes. Thermal diffusivity is α = 1. At each step, the explicit five-point stencil updates an interior node from its old value and the four adjacent values.')
equation(report, 'u(new) = (1 − 4r)u(center) + r[u(left) + u(right) + u(bottom) + u(top)]')
body(report, 'Here r = Δt/h². The stable solver accepts 0 < r ≤ 0.25. For a requested final time T, it rounds the step count up and divides T by that count so the run reaches T within the requested time-step limit.')
heading(report, 'Cases and implementation')
body(report, 'Cases 1 and 2 start with a cold interior. Case 1 holds all edges at 1. Case 2 holds the top and bottom edges, including their corners, at 1 and the remaining side nodes at 0. Case V starts from sin(πx) sin(πy) with zero edge values.')
body(report, 'The C++17 solver stores each time level in a contiguous row-major array. It holds the edges fixed, updates the interior into the second array, and swaps the arrays after each step. The Makefile uses -O2 and -ffp-contract=off. The reported time covers the step loop. An optional headerless binary file contains the full grid in row-major order.')
heading(report, 'Verification')
body(report, 'For Case V, the exact solution is sin(πx) sin(πy) exp(−2π²t). At T = 0.05 with r = 0.2, the observed order of the discrete L₂ error approached 2 across N = 16, 32, 64, 128, and 256 (Appendix D). The separate instability run used r = 0.2625, 5% above the limit. The error became visible near the center of the plate and developed into a checkerboard (Appendix E). The verified serial output supplies the reference field for the MPI implementations.')

heading(report, 'Appendix D Serial convergence study', page=True)
body(report, 'Case V used T = 0.05 and r = 0.2 at each grid resolution. The discrete L₂ error is h times the square root of the sum of squared nodal errors. We calculate the observed order from consecutive L₂ errors as log₂[E(h)/E(h/2)].')
convergence_table(report, 'Table D1. Case V at the final time T = 0.05.')
figure(report, convergence_plot, 6.35, 'Figure D1. Log-log convergence of both error norms. The reference line has slope 2.',
       'Both Case V error curves follow a reference line of order two as the grid is refined.')
body(report, 'The first refinement gave an observed order of 2.0058 and the last gave 2.0001. This agrees with the expected second-order convergence in h when Δt is proportional to h².')

heading(report, 'Appendix E Deliberate instability test', page=True)
body(report, 'The experimental program ran Case V with N = 16 and r = 0.2625. At about 400 steps, the largest visible errors were in the interior near the center. By 500 steps the maximum error exceeded 1. The saved snapshots show this pattern by 400 steps without giving an exact onset iteration.')
instability_table(report, 'Table E1. Maximum error at each saved step count.')
figure(report, intermediate_plot, 5.86, 'Figure E1. Error fields at 400 and 500 steps. Each panel has its own color scale.',
       'Two numerical error fields show alternating signed errors concentrated near the plate center.')
figure(report, final_plot, 3.10, 'Figure E2. The field after 1,000 steps has alternating positive and negative temperatures.',
       'The final field at 1,000 steps shows an alternating red and blue checkerboard.')
body(report, 'The five-point update amplifies high-frequency modes when r exceeds 0.25. A negative amplification factor reverses the error sign each step, which explains the alternating pattern.')

heading(report, 'Appendix F Reproducing the serial tests', page=True)
body(report, 'Run the commands from part1/ on Ubuntu. Python 3 and Matplotlib are required for the plots.')
commands(report, 'make\n'
    'for N in 16 32 64 128 256; do\n'
    '    ./heat --decomp serial --case V --N "$N" --r 0.2 --T 0.05\n'
    'done\n'
    'python3 scripts/plot_convergence.py')
body(report, 'The convergence plotting script reads the recorded solver output in results/convergence_runs.txt and regenerates the table and plot.')
commands(report, './heat --decomp serial --case 1 --N 64 --r 0.2 --steps 100 \\\n'
    '    --out results/demo_case1.bin\n'
    './heat --decomp serial --case 2 --N 64 --r 0.2 --steps 100 \\\n'
    '    --out results/demo_case2.bin\n'
    './heat --decomp serial --case V --N 16 --r 0.2625 --steps 1000')
body(report, 'The last command checks that the stable solver rejects r = 0.2625 with an error message.')
commands(report, 'make unstable_demo\n'
    './unstable_demo --case V --N 16 --r 0.2625 --steps 1000 \\\n'
    '    --out results/instability_1000.bin\n'
    'python3 scripts/plot_instability.py')
body(report, 'The benchmark and figure values are based on the Part 1 solver logs and verification fields. The test definitions are given in the project specification, Section 5, and in Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation, Tasks 6.7 and 7.2.')
report.save(output / 'Part1_Report_Copy_Ready.docx')

numerical = new_document('Part 1 Numerical solutions')
numerical.add_heading('Part 1 Numerical solutions', 0)
body(numerical, 'These solutions derive the serial heat update, its stability limit, and its expected convergence order. The Case V results verify the predicted second-order convergence when the time step scales with h².')

heading(numerical, 'Task 3.2 Central second difference')
body(numerical, '(a) Add the Taylor expansions at x + h and x − h. The odd powers cancel.')
equation(numerical, 'f(x + h) + f(x − h) = 2f(x) + h² f″(x) + (h⁴/12) f⁽⁴⁾(x) + O(h⁶)')
body(numerical, '(b) Subtract 2f(x) and divide by h². The leading error is proportional to h², so the approximation is second-order accurate.')
equation(numerical, 'D₂f(x) = [f(x − h) − 2f(x) + f(x + h)] / h²')
equation(numerical, 'D₂f(x) − f″(x) = (h²/12) f⁽⁴⁾(x) + O(h⁴)')
body(numerical, '(c) For f(x) = sin(x) at x = 1, compare D₂f(1) with the exact value −sin(1).')
data_table(numerical, ('h', 'Absolute error', 'Previous error divided by current error'), [
    ('0.1', '0.000700992', '—'), ('0.05', '0.000175292', '3.999'),
    ('0.025', '0.000043826', '4.000'), ('0.0125', '0.000010957', '4.000'),
], (1.05, 1.78, 3.42))

heading(numerical, 'Task 3.4 Discrete Laplacian')
body(numerical, '(a–c) Apply the central second difference separately along x and y, then add the two approximations.')
equation(numerical, 'Dₓₓu = [u(i−1,j) − 2u(i,j) + u(i+1,j)] / Δx²')
equation(numerical, 'Dᵧᵧu = [u(i,j−1) − 2u(i,j) + u(i,j+1)] / Δy²')
body(numerical, 'The sum approximates ∇²u with error O(Δx² + Δy²).')
body(numerical, '(d–e) With Δx = Δy = h, the stencil below is divided by h².')
commands(numerical, '                 +1\n'
    '          +1     −4     +1\n'
    '                 +1')
body(numerical, '(f) The formula is 4/h² times the neighboring average minus the center. A center cooler than its neighbors gives a positive Laplacian. This is the same local comparison as the mean-value formula.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 5.2 Explicit two dimensional update', page=True)
body(numerical, '(a) Forward Euler in time and the discrete Laplacian in space give the update at an interior node.')
equation(numerical, 'uⁿ⁺¹(i,j) = uⁿ(i,j) + αΔt [Dₓₓuⁿ(i,j) + Dᵧᵧuⁿ(i,j)]')
body(numerical, '(b) For equal spacing h, define r = αΔt/h². The center coefficient is 1 − 4r, and each of the four neighbors has weight r.')
equation(numerical, 'uⁿ⁺¹(i,j) = (1 − 4r)uⁿ(i,j) + r[uⁿ(i−1,j) + uⁿ(i+1,j) + uⁿ(i,j−1) + uⁿ(i,j+1)]')
body(numerical, '(c) The stencil takes all five inputs from level n and writes one center value at level n + 1.')
commands(numerical, 'Old level n                          New level n + 1\n'
    '          Top\n'
    'Left     Center     Right    ────>        Center\n'
    '         Bottom')
body(numerical, '(d) With the coefficients precomputed, one update takes three additions to sum the neighbors, two multiplications and one final addition. It reads five grid values and writes one. Neighboring updates can reuse values in cache.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 5.5 Truncation error and consistency')
body(numerical, 'Insert the smooth exact solution into the discrete equation. Define τ as the forward time difference minus α times the two centered space differences. Taylor expansion at the current node and time gives')
equation(numerical, 'Forward time difference = uₜ + (Δt/2)uₜₜ + O(Δt²)')
equation(numerical, 'Dₓₓu = uₓₓ + (Δx²/12)uₓₓₓₓ + O(Δx⁴)')
equation(numerical, 'Dᵧᵧu = uᵧᵧ + (Δy²/12)uᵧᵧᵧᵧ + O(Δy⁴)')
body(numerical, 'The terms uₜ − α(uₓₓ + uᵧᵧ) cancel because the exact solution satisfies the heat equation. The leading terms that remain are')
equation(numerical, 'τ = (Δt/2)uₜₜ − (αΔx²/12)uₓₓₓₓ − (αΔy²/12)uᵧᵧᵧᵧ + O(Δt² + Δx⁴ + Δy⁴).')
body(numerical, 'Therefore τ = O(Δt) + O(Δx²) + O(Δy²), so p = 1 and q = 2. The error tends to zero as all steps shrink, which establishes consistency.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 6.3 Two dimensional stability')
body(numerical, '(a) Substitute the mode Gⁿ exp[i(jθ + kφ)]. A shift by one node multiplies the mode by exp(±iθ) or exp(±iφ). Pairing the shifts gives the sine-squared form below.')
equation(numerical, 'G = 1 − 4rₓ sin²(θ/2) − 4rᵧ sin²(φ/2),  rₓ = αΔt/Δx²,  rᵧ = αΔt/Δy²')
body(numerical, '(b) G is at most 1 and is smallest for θ = φ = π, the alternating mode. Requiring that minimum to be at least −1 gives the stability condition.')
equation(numerical, 'αΔt(1/Δx² + 1/Δy²) ≤ 1/2')
body(numerical, '(c–d) For equal spacing, the bound is r ≤ 1/4 in 2D. The corresponding equal-spacing limits are 1/2 in 1D and 1/6 in 3D.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 6.7 Deliberate instability', page=True)
body(numerical, 'We ran Case V at N = 16 and r = 0.2625, which is 5% above the 2D stability limit. The Fourier bound gives G = 1 − 8r = −1.1 for the ideal alternating mode. The minus sign reverses the mode each step and |G| > 1 amplifies it.')
body(numerical, 'At 300 steps the maximum error was still small. In the saved 400-step field, the new oscillations are most visible in the interior near the plate center. At 500 steps the maximum error exceeds 1, and at 1,000 steps the checkerboard dominates the field. The snapshots show the region where instability became visible without resolving its exact onset step.')
instability_table(numerical, 'Maximum absolute error at the saved step counts.')
figure(numerical, intermediate_plot, 5.68, 'Error fields at 400 and 500 steps. Each panel has its own color scale.',
       'Intermediate error fields show oscillations growing in the interior near the center.')
figure(numerical, final_plot, 3.28, 'Temperature field after 1,000 steps at r = 0.2625.',
       'Final field shows an alternating grid-scale checkerboard with very large signed values.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 7.1 Expected convergence order', page=True)
body(numerical, 'Task 5.5 gives error terms of order Δt and h². Keeping r fixed makes Δt = rh²/α, so both terms scale as h². For the stable, smooth verification problem we expect second-order convergence in h. Halving h should reduce the error by about a factor of four.')

heading(numerical, 'Task 7.2 Convergence study')
body(numerical, '(a) The serial solver ran Case V to T = 0.05 for N = 16, 32, 64, 128, and 256 with r = 0.2. At each of these grid sizes, T/(rh²) is an integer and r is below the stability limit.')
body(numerical, '(b) At T we compared each grid with the exact field sin(πx) sin(πy) exp(−2π²T). We report the maximum absolute nodal error and the discrete L₂ error, h times the square root of the sum of squared nodal errors.')
convergence_table(numerical, 'Measured Case V errors and observed L₂ orders.')
figure(numerical, convergence_plot, 6.20, 'Log-log plot of the two measured errors against grid spacing h, with a second-order reference line.',
       'The measured maximum and discrete L2 errors decrease in parallel with an order-two trend.')
body(numerical, '(c) Using p = log₂[E(h)/E(h/2)] for the discrete L₂ error, the orders approach 2 from 2.0058 to 2.0001. The measured behavior agrees with the prediction in Task 7.1.')
body(numerical, 'Sources. Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation, Tasks 3.2, 3.4, 5.2, 5.5, 6.3, 6.7, 7.1, and 7.2. The measured values and plots come from the Part 1 solver logs and verification fields.')
numerical.save(output / 'Part1_Numerical_Solutions.docx')
print('\n'.join(str(p) for p in sorted(output.glob('*.docx'))))

    $wordApp = New-Object -ComObject Word.Application -ErrorAction Stop
    $wordApp.Visible = $false
    $wordApp.DisplayAlerts = 0
    foreach ($stem in @('Part1_Report_Copy_Ready','Part1_Numerical_Solutions')) {
        $docxFile = Join-Path $outputDir "$stem.docx"
        $pdfFile = Join-Path $qaDir "$stem.pdf"
        $draftDoc = $wordApp.Documents.Open($docxFile, $false, $true, $false)
        try { $draftDoc.ExportAsFixedFormat($pdfFile, 17) }
        finally { $draftDoc.Close(0) }
        Get-Item -LiteralPath $pdfFile | Select-Object Name,Length
    }
} finally {
    if ($null -ne $wordApp) { $wordApp.Quit(0) }
}`,

from lxml import etree

  assert not any(n.startswith(('word/header','word/footer')) for n in z.namelist())
  ns={'w':'http://schemas.openxmlformats.org/wordprocessingml/2006/main'}
  xml=etree.fromstring(z.read('word/document.xml'))

  assert not xml.xpath('//w:sectPr/w:headerReference|//w:sectPr/w:footerReference',namespaces=ns)

const s=String.raw`
import hashlib
import os
from pathlib import Path
from docx import Document
from docx.shared import Pt

source = Path(os.environ['PART1_WORD_REPORT'])
out = Path(os.environ['PART1_REPORT_QA'])
print('Original SHA256', hashlib.sha256(source.read_bytes()).hexdigest())
doc = Document(source)
paragraphs = doc.paragraphs
start = next(i for i, p in enumerate(paragraphs) if p.text == 'Appendix F Reproducing the serial tests')
for p in paragraphs[start + 1:]:
    p._element.getparent().remove(p._element)

def heading(text):
    doc.add_heading(text, 2)

def body(text):
    doc.add_paragraph(text)

def commands(text):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.paragraph_format.keep_together = True
    p.paragraph_format.line_spacing = 1.00
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(6)
    run = p.add_run(text)
    run.font.name = 'Consolas'
    run.font.size = Pt(9)
    with (out / 'commands.sh').open('a', encoding='utf-8', newline='\n') as script:
        script.write(text + '\n\n')

body('Start in the part1 directory on Ubuntu, using Bash. The plotting scripts need Python 3 and Matplotlib. Each run saves its results in a new temporary folder.')
heading('Build both programs')
commands('make heat unstable_demo\n'
         'part1="$PWD"\n'
         'run_dir=$(mktemp -d)\n'
         'cd "$run_dir"\n'
         'mkdir results')
heading('Case V convergence')
commands('for N in 16 32 64 128 256; do\n'
         '    "$part1/heat" --case V --N "$N" --r 0.2 --T 0.05\n'
         'done > results/convergence_runs.txt\n'
         'python3 "$part1/scripts/plot_convergence.py"')
body('The script reads the new log and creates convergence.csv and a log-log plot of the discrete L₂ error. The observed order should approach 2.')
heading('Fixed boundaries and stability check')
commands('for c in 1 2; do\n'
         '    "$part1/heat" --case "$c" --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 20 --debug\n'
         'done\n'
         '"$part1/heat" --case V --N 16 --r 0.2625 --steps 1000')
body('The printed grids show the fixed boundaries and temperatures in [0, 1]. The final command is expected to fail because r = 0.2625 exceeds the stable limit.')
heading('Deliberate instability')
commands('demo_args=(--case V --N 16 --r 0.2625)\n'
         'for n in 100 300 400 500 600 1000; do\n'
         '    field="results/instability_$n.bin"\n'
         '    log="results/instability_$n.txt"\n'
         '    "$part1/unstable_demo" "${demo_args[@]}" --steps "$n" --out "$field" > "$log"\n'
         'done\n'
         'python3 "$part1/scripts/plot_instability.py"\n'
         'printf \'Results are in %s/results\\n\' "$run_dir"\n'
         'cd "$part1"')
body('The logs give the errors in Table E1. The plotting script creates the 1,000-step checkerboard. Figure E1 uses the 400- and 500-step fields with the exact Case V solution subtracted at their respective final times.')

doc.save(out / source.name)
print('Corrected report', out / source.name)
`;
store("appendixF_builder",s);

# Appendix F Reproducing the serial tests

Start in the part1 directory on Ubuntu, using Bash. The plotting scripts need Python 3 and Matplotlib. Each run saves its results in a new temporary folder.

## Build both programs

make heat unstable_demo\
&#x20;part1="$PWD"\
&#x20;run_dir=$(mktemp -d)\
&#x20;cd "$run_dir"\
&#x20;mkdir results

## Case V convergence

for N in 16 32 64 128 256; do\
&#x20;    "$part1/heat" --case V --N "$N" --r 0.2 --T 0.05\
&#x20;done > results/convergence_runs.txt\
&#x20;python3 "$part1/scripts/plot_convergence.py"



The script reads the new log and creates convergence.csv and a log-log plot of the discrete L₂ error. The observed order should approach 2.

## Fixed boundaries and stability check

for c in 1 2; do\
&#x20;    "$part1/heat" --case "$c" --N 8 --r 0.2 --steps 20 --debug\
&#x20;done\
&#x20;"$part1/heat" --case V --N 16 --r 0.2625 --steps 1000



The printed grids show the fixed boundaries and temperatures in [0, 1]. The final command is expected to fail because r = 0.2625 exceeds the stable limit.

## Deliberate instability

demo_args=(--case V --N 16 --r 0.2625)\
&#x20;for n in 100 300 400 500 600 1000; do\
&#x20;    field="results/instability\_$n.bin"\
&#x20;    log="results/instability\_$n.txt"\
&#x20;    "$part1/unstable_demo" "${demo_args[@]}" --steps "$n" --out "$field" > "$log"\
&#x20;done\
&#x20;python3 "$part1/scripts/plot_instability.py"\
&#x20;printf 'Results are in %s/results\n' "$run_dir"\
&#x20;cd "$part1"



The logs give the errors in Table E1. The plotting script creates the 1,000-step checkerboard. Figure E1 uses the 400- and 500-step fields with the exact Case V solution subtracted at their respective final times.

1.4       What the Laplacian means
The Laplacian has a geometric interpretation that is worth carrying with you into the rest of the
course. For a smooth function u in two dimensions, the average of u over a small circle of radius
r centred at (x, y) satisfies
                    Z 2π
                1                                                       r2 2
                           u(x + r cos ϕ, y + r sin ϕ) dϕ = u(x, y) +     ∇ u(x, y) + O(r4 ).     (1.2)
               2π    0                                                  4

So ∇2 u(x, y) is, up to a positive factor, how much the average of the surroundings exceeds the
value at the point. Read the heat equation with this in mind: a point heats up (ut > 0) when its
surroundings are on average warmer than it, and cools down when they are colder.
1.5. INITIAL AND BOUNDARY CONDITIONS                                                                 3

  Definition 1.4. (Initial condition)

  Definition 1.5. (Boundary conditions)
  On the boundary ∂Ω one of the following is prescribed for t > 0:

    Definition 1.2. (Thermal diffusivity)

    The constant α = k/(ρc), measured in m2 /s, is the thermal diffusivity of the material. It
    measures how quickly temperature differences even out: a large α means heat spreads fast
    relative to how much energy the material can store.


                           Material (room temperature)     α (m2 /s, approximate)
                           Copper                                 1.1 × 10−4
                           Aluminium                              9.7 × 10−5
                           Carbon steel                           1.3 × 10−5
                           Air                                    2.2 × 10−5
                           Water                                  1.4 × 10−7


1.3       The heat equation in two dimensions
The same argument applied to a small rectangle of the plate, with heat crossing all four sides,
leads to ρc ∂T /∂t = ∇ · (k∇T ). For constant k this is the equation we will solve throughout the
project. From now on we write u for the temperature.

    Definition 1.3. (The 2D heat equation)

    On a domain Ω ⊂ R2 and for t > 0,
                                               2
                                               ∂ u ∂2u
                                                       
                                      ∂u
                                         =α        + 2 = α ∇2 u.                                (1.1)
                                      ∂t       ∂x2  ∂y

    The operator ∇2 = ∂ 2 /∂x2 + ∂ 2 /∂y 2 is the Laplacian, also written ∆. It is a scalar: the
    trace of the Hessian matrix of second derivatives. (In optimization and machine learning
    ∇2 f often denotes the full Hessian; in these notes it always means the Laplacian.)



1.7     Scales and dimensionless form
  Task 1.2. (Dimensional analysis)

    (a) Show that the combination αt/L2 is dimensionless, where L is a length.
    (b) Using only α and L, form the unique combination τ that has units of time. Explain
        why any time it takes heat to spread across a distance L must be proportional to τ .
        Compute τ for a copper plate and for a steel plate with L = 10 cm. Does the answer
        match your intuition?
4                                                          CHAPTER 1. THE PHYSICAL MODEL


     (c) Define the dimensionless variables x̂ = x/L, ŷ = y/L, t̂ = αt/L2 (the hats distinguish
         these pure numbers from the physical coordinates). Using the chain rule, show that
         (1.1) becomes ut̂ = ux̂x̂ + uŷŷ , in which neither α nor L appears. Conclude that a single
         solver written for α = 1 on a domain of size one covers every plate size and every
         material.

   Keep part (a) in mind. When you later discretize with a grid spacing and a time step, a
similar dimensionless combination will appear, and it will control the behaviour of your scheme.


1.8       Exact solutions by separation of variables
For the rectangle with u = 0 on all four edges, the heat equation has exact solutions of a very
simple form. These are invaluable for testing code.

    Task 1.3. (Fourier modes)
    Let m, n be positive integers. Show that
                                        mπx   nπy     h        m2    n2  i
                    umn (x, y, t) = sin       sin      exp − απ 2       +     t                    (1.3)
                                          Lx      Ly                L2x   L2y

    satisfies (1.1) and vanishes on all four edges of [0, Lx ] × [0, Ly ]. What is its initial condition?
                              e − e−iθ = 1 − i ν sin θ,      |G|2 = 1 + ν 2 sin2 θ.
                                      
                 G=1−
                            2
 So |G| > 1 for every θ ̸= 0, ±π and every ν > 0: the scheme is unstable whatever time step
 we choose, even though it is consistent. By the Lax theorem, it does not converge.

  Replace the central difference by the backward (“upwind”) difference:

                                   un+1 = unj − ν unj − unj−1 .

const py=String.raw`
import json, math, os
from pathlib import Path
import matplotlib
matplotlib.use('Agg')
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from matplotlib.ticker import LogLocator, NullFormatter

work = Path(os.environ['WHOLE_NUM_QA'])
roundoff = []
for p in range(1, 11):
    h = 10.0**(-p)
    d2 = (math.sin(1-h) - 2*math.sin(1) + math.sin(1+h))/h**2
    roundoff.append([p, h, abs(d2 + math.sin(1))])

def solve(steps, backward):
    dt = 1.0/steps
    u = 1.0
    for _ in range(steps):
        if backward:
            u /= 1 + 50*dt
        else:
            u *= 1 - 50*dt
    return u

stability = []
for n in (50, 26, 25, 24, 20):
    dt = 1.0/n
    stability.append([n, dt, 1 - 50*dt, abs(solve(n, False)), solve(n, True)])

euler = []
exact = math.exp(-50.0)
for n in (8000, 16000, 32000, 64000, 128000):
    error = abs(solve(n, False) - exact)
    order = math.log2(euler[-1][2]/error) if euler else None
    euler.append([n, 1.0/n, error, order])
assert min(roundoff, key=lambda row: row[-1])[0] == 4
assert stability[2][2:4] == [-1.0, 1.0]
assert all(euler[i][2] < euler[i-1][2] for i in range(1, len(euler)))
(work / 'numerical_data.json').write_text(json.dumps(dict(roundoff=roundoff, stability=stability, euler=euler)), encoding='utf-8')

plt.rcParams.update({'font.size': 10.8, 'axes.spines.top': False, 'axes.spines.right': False})
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6.6, 2.65), layout='constrained')
h = [r[1] for r in roundoff]
errors = [r[2] for r in roundoff]
ax.loglog(h, errors, 'o-', lw=1.7, color='#176078')
ax.scatter([h[3]], [errors[3]], color='#a45421', s=45, zorder=4, label='Smallest sampled error')
ax.set(xlabel='Grid spacing h', ylabel='Absolute error')
ax.set_xticks([10.0**k for k in range(-10, 0)])
ax.xaxis.set_minor_formatter(NullFormatter())
ax.grid(which='major', alpha=.19)
ax.legend(frameon=False, loc='upper right')
fig.savefig(work/'roundoff.png', dpi=220)
plt.close(fig)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6.6, 2.62), layout='constrained')
dt = [r[1] for r in euler]
err = [r[2] for r in euler]
ax.loglog(dt, err, 'o-', lw=1.7, color='#176078', label='Forward Euler')
ax.loglog(dt, [err[-1] * d / dt[-1] for d in dt], '--', color='#707070', label='Slope 1')
ax.set(xlabel='Time step Δt', ylabel='Absolute error at t = 1')
ax.grid(which='major', alpha=.20)
ax.legend(frameon=False, loc='upper left')
fig.savefig(work/'euler.png', dpi=220)
plt.close(fig)
print(work)
`;
store("whole_num_experiment",py);

const p=String.raw`
from pathlib import Path
from zipfile import ZipFile
from io import BytesIO
from statistics import median
import csv
import json
import math
import os
import re
from docx import Document
from docx.enum.table import WD_TABLE_ALIGNMENT, WD_CELL_VERTICAL_ALIGNMENT
from docx.enum.text import WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH
from docx.oxml import OxmlElement
from docx.oxml.ns import qn
from docx.shared import Inches, Pt, RGBColor

qa = Path(os.environ['WHOLE_NUM_QA'])
root = Path.cwd()
desktop = Path('C:/Users/User/Desktop')
results = root / 'part1/results'
data = json.loads((qa/'numerical_data.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
reference = desktop/'Part1_Report_Section_and_Appendices.docx'
with ZipFile(reference) as report:
    convergence_plot = report.read('word/media/image1.png')
    intermediate_plot = report.read('word/media/image2.png')
    instability_plot = report.read('word/media/image3.png')

doc = Document()
sec = doc.sections[0]
sec.page_width, sec.page_height = Inches(8.27), Inches(11.69)
sec.left_margin = sec.right_margin = Inches(.73)
sec.top_margin = sec.bottom_margin = Inches(.63)
doc.core_properties.title = 'Numerical Solutions for the Parallel Heat Diffusion Project'
doc.core_properties.author = ''
for name, pts, bold, space_before, space_after in [
    ('Normal', 10.5, False, 0, 5),
    ('Title', 17, True, 0, 8),
    ('Heading 1', 13.5, True, 7, 6),
    ('Heading 2', 11.5, True, 7, 4),
    ('Caption', 9, False, 2, 5),
]:
    sty = doc.styles[name]
    sty.font.name = 'Aptos'
    sty.font.size = Pt(pts)
    sty.font.bold = bold
    sty.font.italic = False
    sty.font.color.rgb = RGBColor(0,0,0)
    fmt = sty.paragraph_format
    fmt.space_before, fmt.space_after = Pt(space_before), Pt(space_after)
    fmt.line_spacing = 1.08 if name == 'Normal' else 1.03
    fmt.widow_control = True
    if name == 'Title' or name.startswith('Heading'):
        fmt.keep_with_next = True
        fmt.keep_together = True
    props = sty.element.get_or_add_pPr()
    for border in props.findall(qn('w:pBdr')):
        props.remove(border)

def body(text):
    return doc.add_paragraph(text)

def heading(text, level=1, page=False):
    p = doc.add_heading(text, level)
    p.paragraph_format.page_break_before = page
    return p

def task(num, title, page=False):
    return heading(f'Task {num} {title}', 2, page)

def eq(text):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(4)
    p.paragraph_format.keep_together = True
    p.paragraph_format.left_indent = Inches(.17)
    for token in re.split(r'([_^]\{[^{}]+\})', text):
        if not token:
            continue
        formatted = token.startswith(('_{','^{'))
        run = p.add_run(token[2:-1] if formatted else token)
        run.font.name = 'Cambria Math'
        if formatted:
            run.font.subscript = token.startswith('_')
            run.font.superscript = token.startswith('^')
    return p

def code(text):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.paragraph_format.keep_together = True
    p.paragraph_format.line_spacing = 1.01
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(6)
    run = p.add_run(text)
    run.font.name = 'Consolas'
    run.font.size = Pt(9.2)

def caption(text, before=False):
    p = doc.add_paragraph(text, 'Caption')
    p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = before
    return p

def node(parent, tag, **attrs):
    child = OxmlElement('w:'+tag)
    for attr, val in attrs.items():
        child.set(qn('w:'+attr), str(val))
    parent.append(child)
    return child

def table(labels, rows, widths, title=None):
    if title:
        caption(title, before=True)
    t = doc.add_table(rows=0, cols=len(labels))
    t.autofit = False
    t.alignment = WD_TABLE_ALIGNMENT.CENTER
    for col, width in zip(t.columns, widths):
        col.width = Inches(width)
    p = t._tbl.tblPr
    borders = node(p, 'tblBorders')
    for e in ('top','bottom','left','right','insideH','insideV'):
        node(borders,e,val='single',color='D9D9D9',sz=4)
    margins = node(p,'tblCellMar')
    for e in ('top','bottom','left','right'):
        node(margins,e,w=34 if e in ('top','bottom') else 80,type='dxa')
    for i, row in enumerate([labels, *rows]):
        wr = t.add_row()
        trp = wr._tr.get_or_add_trPr()
        node(trp,'cantSplit')
        if i == 0:
            node(trp,'tblHeader')
        for j, (c, value) in enumerate(zip(wr.cells,row)):
            c.width = Inches(widths[j])
            c.vertical_alignment = WD_CELL_VERTICAL_ALIGNMENT.CENTER
            if i == 0:
                node(c._tc.get_or_add_tcPr(),'shd',fill='F2F2F2')
            cp = c.paragraphs[0]
            cp.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(0)
            cp.paragraph_format.line_spacing = 1.03
            cp.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = i == 0
            cp.alignment = WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.CENTER if i == 0 else WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.RIGHT
            run = cp.add_run(str(value))
            run.bold = i == 0
            run.font.size = Pt(9.4)
    return t

def figure(data, width, title, alt):
    p = doc.add_paragraph()
    p.alignment = WD_ALIGN_PARAGRAPH.CENTER
    p.paragraph_format.keep_with_next = True
    p.paragraph_format.space_before = Pt(3)
    p.paragraph_format.space_after = Pt(0)
    source = BytesIO(data) if isinstance(data, bytes) else str(data)
    pic = p.add_run().add_picture(source, width=Inches(width))
    pic._inline.docPr.set('descr',alt)
    caption(title)

def sci(x, digits=4):
    if x == 0:
        return '0'
    a,b = f'{x:.{digits}e}'.split('e')
    return a + ' × 10' + str(int(b)).translate(str.maketrans('-0123456789','⁻⁰¹²³⁴⁵⁶⁷⁸⁹'))

def section(n, text, page=True):
    heading(f'Chapter {n} {text}', page=page)

doc.add_heading('Numerical Solutions for the Parallel Heat Diffusion Project', 0)
body('Parallel 2D Heat Diffusion with MPI over Tailscale')
body('The solutions follow the numbering of Numerical Methods for the Heat Equation. They derive the finite-difference solver, check its stability and accuracy, and apply the communication model to the MPI experiments.')

section(1, 'The physical model', page=False)
task('1.1', 'Mean value formula')
body('Write c = cos φ and s = sin φ. Expand around (x, y), evaluating every derivative at the center.')
eq('u(x + ρc, y + ρs) = u + ρ(cu_{x} + su_{y}) + (ρ²/2)(c²u_{xx} + 2csu_{xy} + s²u_{yy}) + O(ρ³).')
body('Averaging over a full circle gives zero for c, s, and cs, and 1/2 for c² and s². The cubic terms also average to zero because each changes sign when φ is replaced by φ + π. For a smooth u, the remaining error is O(ρ⁴).')
eq('(1/2π)∫₀²π u(x + ρ cos φ, y + ρ sin φ) dφ = u(x,y) + (ρ²/4)∇²u(x,y) + O(ρ⁴).')
body('This is formula (1.2). The radius is written ρ here to distinguish it from the solver parameter r.')

task('1.2', 'Diffusion scales')
body('(a) Thermal diffusivity α has units m²/s, so αt/L² has units (m²/s)·s/m² = 1.')
body('(b) In αᵃLᵇ, the time exponent is −a and the length exponent is 2a + b. Setting these to 1 and 0 gives a = −1 and b = 2. The characteristic time is τ = L²/α, multiplied by a dimensionless factor that depends on geometry and the definition of “spread.”')
table(('Material','α (m²/s)','τ for L = 0.10 m'),[
    ('Copper','1.1 × 10⁻⁴','90.9 s'),
    ('Carbon steel','1.3 × 10⁻⁵','769 s'),
],(1.85,1.65,2.20), 'Diffusivities from the background notes.')
body('Copper has the larger diffusivity and the shorter diffusion time. The steel time scale is about 8.5 times as long.')
body('(c) Set x̂ = x/L, ŷ = y/L, and t̂ = αt/L². The chain rule yields the equation below after division by α/L².')
eq('∂u/∂t̂ = ∂²u/∂x̂² + ∂²u/∂ŷ².')
body('A unit-square solver with α = 1 applies to square plates of other sizes and materials when the spatial and time coordinates and the prescribed data are rescaled.')

task('1.3', 'Fourier modes')
body('Let kₓ = mπ/Lₓ, kᵧ = nπ/Lᵧ, and σ = α(kₓ² + kᵧ²). For the mode below, uₜ = −σu, while uₓₓ = −kₓ²u and uᵧᵧ = −kᵧ²u. Hence uₜ = α(uₓₓ + uᵧᵧ).')
eq('u(x,y,t) = sin(k_{x}x) sin(k_{y}y) exp(−σt).')
body('Each edge makes at least one sine factor zero because m and n are integers. The initial condition is sin(kₓx) sin(kᵧy). Case V uses m = n = 1 on the unit square.')
`;
store("num_part_a",p);
text(p.length);

const p=String.raw`
section(2, 'Taylor expansions')
task('2.1', 'Expansions at nearby points')
body('All derivatives on the right are evaluated at x. Keep terms through fourth order.')
eq('(a)  f(x+h) = f(x) + hf′ + (h²/2)f″ + (h³/6)f‴ + (h⁴/24)f^{(4)} + O(h⁵).')
eq('(b)  f(x−h) = f(x) − hf′ + (h²/2)f″ − (h³/6)f‴ + (h⁴/24)f^{(4)} + O(h⁵).')
body('The odd powers change sign because replacing h by −h multiplies the kth term by (−1)ᵏ.')
eq('(c)  f(x+2h) = f(x) + 2hf′ + 2h²f″ + (4h³/3)f‴ + (2h⁴/3)f^{(4)} + O(h⁵).')

section(3, 'Finite differences', page=False)
task('3.1', 'Backward difference')
body('Substitute the expansion of f(x − h) into the numerator and divide by h.')
eq('D₋f(x) = [f(x) − f(x−h)]/h = f′(x) − (h/2)f″(x) + O(h²).')
body('The leading error D₋f − f′ is −(h/2)f″. The backward difference is first-order accurate.')

task('3.2', 'Central second difference')
body('(a–b) Add the expansions at x + h and x − h. The odd terms cancel. After subtracting 2f(x) and dividing by h², the result is')
eq('D₂f(x) = [f(x−h) − 2f(x) + f(x+h)]/h².')
eq('D₂f(x) − f″(x) = (h²/12) f^{(4)}(x) + O(h⁴).')
body('The leading error is proportional to h², so the approximation is second-order accurate.')
body('(c) For f(x) = sin(x) at x = 1, the exact second derivative is −sin(1).')
hvals = (0.1, 0.05, 0.025, 0.0125)
err = [abs((math.sin(1-h) - 2*math.sin(1) + math.sin(1+h))/h**2 + math.sin(1)) for h in hvals]
table(('h','Absolute error','Previous error ÷ current error'),[
    (f'{h:g}',f'{e:.9f}',f'{err[i-1]/e:.3f}' if i else '—')
    for i,(h,e) in enumerate(zip(hvals,err))
], (1,1.65,3.04))
body('Halving h reduces the error by about a factor of four, as expected.')

task('3.3', 'Checks on the formula')
body('(a) A constant gives numerator 1 − 2 + 1 = 0. A linear function gives (x−h) − 2x + (x+h) = 0. For x², the numerator is (x−h)² − 2x² + (x+h)² = 2h², so D₂x² = 2. The formula exactly captures the curvature of these polynomials.')
body('(b) The weights 1, −2, and 1 sum to zero. Adding the same constant to all values leaves the second difference unchanged.')
body('(c) D₂f equals 2/h² times the average of the two neighbors minus the center. A center below the neighboring average gives a positive result.')

task('3.4', 'Discrete Laplacian', page=True)
body('(a–c) Apply the centered second difference along each coordinate. Their sum approximates ∇²u with truncation error O(Δx² + Δy²).')
eq('D_{xx}u = [u(i−1,j) − 2u(i,j) + u(i+1,j)] / Δx².')
eq('D_{yy}u = [u(i,j−1) − 2u(i,j) + u(i,j+1)] / Δy².')
body('(d–e) When Δx = Δy = h, the Laplacian uses the following weights with a common factor 1/h².')
code('                 +1\n'
     '          +1     −4     +1\n'
     '                 +1')
body('(f) This is 4/h² times the four-neighbor average minus the center. Like the continuous mean-value formula, it gives a positive Laplacian when the surrounding values are warmer than the center.')

task('3.5', 'Round off and grid spacing')
body('We evaluated the three-point formula for sin(x) at x = 1 using double-precision arithmetic. The experiment is reproduced by the following Python loop.')
code('from math import sin\n'
     'for k in range(1, 11):\n'
     '    h = 10.0**(-k)\n'
     '    d2 = (sin(1-h) - 2*sin(1) + sin(1+h)) / h**2\n'
     '    print(h, abs(d2 + sin(1)))')
roundoff = data['roundoff']
table(('h','Absolute error','h','Absolute error'),[
    (sci(a[1],0),sci(a[2]),sci(b[1],0),sci(b[2]))
    for a,b in zip(roundoff[:5],roundoff[5:])
],(1.05,2.0,1.05,2.0), 'Measured errors for the ten spacings.')
figure(qa/'roundoff.png', 6.37, 'The smallest sampled error occurs at h = 10⁻⁴.',
       'Log-log plot showing falling truncation error and rising round-off error as h decreases.')
body('The truncation term scales as h². The subtraction in the numerator loses precision, leaving an error of order ε that is divided by h². Balancing h² and ε/h² gives h of order ε¹ᐟ⁴, about 10⁻⁴ in double precision. Below this, cancellation dominates and the individual errors fluctuate.')

section(4, 'Time discretization')
task('4.1', 'Forward and backward Euler')
body('(a) For u′ = −50u with u(0) = 1, the exact value at t = 1 is exp(−50). We used K steps with Δt = 1/K and iterated both updates in Python.')
code('def euler(steps, backward=False):\n'
     '    dt = 1.0 / steps\n'
     '    u = 1.0\n'
     '    for _ in range(steps):\n'
     '        u = u / (1 + 50*dt) if backward else (1 - 50*dt)*u\n'
     '    return u')
body('(b) Forward Euler multiplies u by 1 − 50Δt. Its magnitude is bounded if that multiplier lies in [−1, 1]. The largest stable step is 0.04. At equality the values alternate between ±1. At K = 24 and K = 20 the magnitudes grow.')
table(('Steps K','Δt','Forward multiplier','Forward |u(1)|','Backward u(1)'),[
    (n, f'{dt:.6f}', f'{g:.6f}', f'{value:.6g}', sci(be,3)) for n,dt,g,value,be in data['stability']
],(.82,1.00,1.68,1.37,1.59),'Stability runs with both updates.')
body('(c) For the finer steps below, we measured the absolute error at t = 1. The observed order log₂(E(Δt)/E(Δt/2)) approaches 1.')
table(('Steps K','Δt','Absolute error at t = 1','Observed order'),[
    (f'{n:,}',sci(dt,3),sci(e),f'{order:.4f}' if order else '—') for n,dt,e,order in data['euler']
],(1.07,1.30,2.02,1.49))
figure(qa/'euler.png',5.85,'The forward Euler errors approach the reference slope of 1.',
       'Log-log plot showing first-order convergence in the forward Euler time step.')
body('(d) Backward Euler gives uⁿ⁺¹ = uⁿ/(1 + 50Δt). The measured values decay even for steps above the forward Euler threshold. Its multiplier is strictly between 0 and 1 for every Δt > 0, so decay holds for any positive time step.')
`;
store("num_part_b",p);text(p.length);
